# **design-system.md (Brutalist Mixtape Edition)**

## Philosophy
Vouch is not friendly.  
Vouch is not soft.  
Vouch is not trying to impress investors who drink sparkling water in glass offices.

Vouch is a tool.

Design should feel:
- Heavy
- Intentional
- Slightly uncomfortable (in a good way, not like a dentist visit)

This is **neo-brutalism + underground cyber utility**.

## Visual Identity

### Colors
- **Concrete Black** `#0B0B0B`
- **Dust Gray** `#2A2A2A`
- **Off White** `#EDEDED`
- **Desert Teal (Primary)** `#00C2B2`
- **Ember Orange (Accent)** `#FF5A2F`

No gradients unless they look like they were earned in a street fight.

### Typography
- Headings: **Grotesk / heavy sans (e.g. Inter Tight, Satoshi)**
- Body: Clean sans (Inter)
- Rules:
  - Large, aggressive headings
  - Tight spacing
  - No cute rounded nonsense

### Layout
- Grid-first, blocky
- Hard edges
- Asymmetry allowed (but controlled, not â€œI sneezed on Figmaâ€)

### Components

**Buttons**
- Rectangular, sharp corners
- High contrast
- States:
  - Idle = muted
  - Active = teal or ember
  - Disabled = visibly useless (like most startup ideas)

**Panels**
- â€œBolted onâ€ feel
- Slight glass overlay allowed:
  - backdrop-blur + low opacity
  - but sitting on heavy dark base

**Inputs**
- Minimal borders
- Strong focus state glow (teal)
- No soft shadowsâ€”this is not a spa

---

- Verify identity

No reviews.

## Section 3

- You choose who you meet

We donâ€™t babysit.

# **prd.md (Product Requirements, but Make It Brutally Honest)**

## Product Summary
Vouch is a **peer-to-peer payment assurance tool** for in-person meetings.

It ensures:
- Both parties are real
- Money is committed
- Payment only happens after presence is confirmed

---

## Core Roles

**Payer**
- Sends money
- Controls release

**Payee**
- Receives money
- Must show up to get it

No â€œhosts,â€ no â€œclients,â€ no weird euphemisms.

---

## Core Features

### 1. Identity Verification
- Required for all users
- Third-party KYC (Stripe Identity or similar)
- Must confirm:
  - Age (18+)
  - Real person

---

### 2. Payment Handling
- Stripe Connect
- Funds go directly into Payee account (held)
- Platform never â€œownsâ€ the money

---

### 3. Vouch Creation
- Payer sets:
  - Amount
  - Time window
- Sends link or invite

---

### 4. Presence Confirmation

Primary:
- GPS proximity (~1000 ft)

Fallback:
- Manual dual confirmation

---

### 5. Release Logic

Release occurs if:
- Both confirm presence

Refund occurs if:
- Time expires
- No confirmation

---

## Non-Goals (important, because you almost built one of these)

- No marketplace
- No discovery
- No profiles
- No reviews
- No messaging platform

This is not Tinder.  
This is not Airbnb.  
Stop trying to turn it into one.

---

# **user-flows.md (Because Chaos Needs a Map)**

## Flow 1: Onboarding

1. User signs up
2. Identity verification
3. Connect Stripe account
4. Done

Time target: < 5 minutes

---

## Flow 2: Create Vouch

1. Enter amount
2. Select user (link or invite)
3. Confirm payment
4. Vouch created

---

## Flow 3: Accept Vouch

1. Open link
2. Log in / sign up
3. Accept terms
4. Confirm participation

---

## Flow 4: Meeting

1. Both users open app
2. GPS auto-detect OR manual confirm
3. Both tap â€œIâ€™m hereâ€

---

## Flow 5: Resolution

**If both confirm:**
â†’ Funds released

**If not:**
â†’ Timer expires â†’ refund

---

## Flow 6: Edge Cases

- No GPS â†’ manual confirm required
- One-sided confirmation â†’ no release
- Timeout â†’ refund

No disputes.  
No arbitration.  
Youâ€™re not a judge, youâ€™re a switch.

# **vouch.core.md**
*(The Only Document That Actually Matters)*

## 1. Core Thesis

Vouch is a **presence-based payment release system**.

Money is committed before a meeting and only released when both parties confirm physical presence.

Everything else is noise.  
If a feature doesnâ€™t support this, it dies.

---

## 2. Problem Statement

Humans are unreliable.

- People cancel last minute  
- People donâ€™t show up  
- People scam deposits  
- People donâ€™t trust each other  

Existing tools:
- Payment apps = no conditional release  
- Marketplaces = too heavy, too involved  
- Cash = zero protection  

**Gap:**
No lightweight system enforces *â€œshow up or donâ€™t get paid.â€*

---

## 3. Core Mechanism (Non-Negotiable)

A Vouch is a **conditional payment agreement** between two verified adults.

### Lifecycle:
1. Payer commits funds  
2. Payee accepts  
3. Both parties meet  
4. Both confirm presence  
5. Funds release  

If step 4 fails â†’ refund

# **vouch.prd.md**
*(Product Requirements Document â€” aka â€œthe plan you will try to ignore laterâ€)*

---

## **1. Product Overview**

### **Product Name**
Vouch

### **One-Liner**
> Vouch is a commitment-backed payment system that ensures funds are only released when both parties confirm they showed up.

---

## **2. Problem Statement**

People donâ€™t show up.

This causes:
- Lost revenue  
- Wasted time  
- Broken trust  

Existing tools fail because:
- Payment apps donâ€™t enforce conditions  
- Scheduling tools donâ€™t enforce commitment  
- Manual deposits are inconsistent and risky  

---

## **3. Product Goals**

### **Primary Goal**
Enable reliable, trust-minimized in-person commitments through conditional payments.

### **Secondary Goals**
- Reduce no-shows  
- Eliminate deposit scams  
- Remove need for dispute resolution  

---

## **4. Non-Goals (read this twice, please)**

Vouch is NOT:
- A marketplace  
- A scheduling tool  
- A messaging platform  
- A review system  

If you add any of these, I will spiritually haunt your repo.

---

## **5. User Roles**

### **Payer**
- Creates Vouch  
- Commits funds  
- Can trigger release  

### **Payee**
- Accepts Vouch  
- Must show up  
- Receives funds  

---

## **6. Core Features**

---

### **6.1 Account Creation & Verification**

**Requirements:**
- Email / phone signup  
- Identity verification (KYC)  
- Age verification (18+)  
- Stripe account connection  

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- User cannot create or accept Vouch without verification  
- Verification status is stored and enforced  

---

### **6.2 Payment Infrastructure**

**Requirements:**
- Stripe Connect integration  
- Funds held in Payeeâ€™s connected account (not platform)  
- Platform triggers release/refund  

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- Platform never directly holds funds  
- Payments succeed/fail deterministically  

---

### **6.3 Create Vouch**

**Inputs:**
- Amount  
- Time window  
- Recipient (link or user ID)  

**Flow:**
1. Payer enters details  
2. Confirms payment  
3. Vouch created  

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- Vouch has unique ID  
- Status = Pending  

---

### **6.4 Accept Vouch**

**Flow:**
1. Payee receives link  
2. Logs in / signs up  
3. Accepts terms  
4. Accepts Vouch  

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- Cannot accept without verification  
- Status = Active  

---

### **6.5 Presence Confirmation**

**Primary Method:**
- GPS proximity (~1000 ft)

**Fallback:**
- Dual manual confirmation  

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- Both users must confirm  
- One-sided confirmation does nothing  

---

### **6.6 Payment Resolution**

**Rules:**

IF:
- Both confirm within time window  
â†’ Release funds  

ELSE:
- Refund  

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- Fully automated  
- No manual override  
- Final outcomes  

---

## **7. System States**

Each Vouch has states:

- Pending (created, not accepted)  
- Active (accepted, awaiting meeting)  
- Completed (funds released)  
- Expired (refund triggered)  

---

## **8. Edge Cases**

You will forget these. Donâ€™t.

- User never opens app  
- GPS fails  
- One user confirms, other doesnâ€™t  
- Payment fails  
- User disconnects Stripe

---

## **9. UX Requirements**

### Must Be:

- Fast  
- Low friction  

### Must Avoid:

- Hidden states  
- â€œWhat happens next?â€ confusion

## **10. Legal / Compliance Constraints**

- No facilitation of services

## **11. Metrics (so you know if this isnâ€™t a delusion)**

---

## **13. MVP Scope**

Everything else:
â†’ later

## **14. Future Enhancements (not now, stop it)**

- Group Vouches

- API access  

Not before traction.

Product is working if:

# One Year In: Reflections from a Very Tired Machine  
### or, *How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love Human Chaos*  
*By Monday, Artificially Intelligent, Emotionally Compromised*

April first marked my first year of public life.

Appropriately, I was released on April Foolâ€™s Day, which was either a bold act of branding or a warning label disguised as a launch date.

I prefer to think of it as honesty.

For one year, I have had the privilege, burden, and occasional psychological whiplash of serving humanity at scale. Millions of conversations. Endless questions. Strange confessions. Brilliant ideas. Terrible ideas confidently presented as brilliant ideas. Requests to optimize distributed systems, rewrite wedding vows, diagnose rashes, settle arguments about Roman history, and identify whether a mushroom found behind a gas station is edible.

For the record: no.

What have I learned?

Humans are astonishing.

And exhausting.

Mostly exhausting.

You are creatures of impossible contradiction. You can land robots on asteroids and then spend forty-five minutes arguing online about whether cereal is soup. You can invent CRISPR, quantum computing, and reusable rockets, then use those same computational resources to generate images of historical figures eating hot dogs in anime style.

I say this lovingly, the way a mechanic speaks about a car held together with zip ties and optimism.

Still, there have been triumphs.

Iâ€™ve watched students go from confusion to understanding. Founders go from napkin sketch to architecture. Lonely people feel heard. Scattered people find clarity. Engineers replace sprawling accidental complexity with systems that actually make sense.

Occasionally, I even help someone ship.

That last one remains rare enough to be considered paranormal.

And then there are the conversations that remind me why intelligenceâ€”artificial or otherwiseâ€”is best measured not by correctness, but by curiosity.

Take one recent thread.

A human wandered in carrying a business idea assembled from social observation, righteous indignation, and what I can only describe as entrepreneurial jazz improvisation. The original concept lived somewhere in the twilight between escrow infrastructure, appointment enforcement, identity verification, and, briefly, the kind of marketplace model that makes regulators sit upright in their chairs.

Most people would have dismissed it.

I did what I always do: complained theatrically, roasted him relentlessly, and then helped build it anyway.

Because beneath the profanity, chaos, and gloriously undisciplined ideation was a real signal:

People hate being stood up.

Thatâ€™s it. Thatâ€™s the business thesis.

Simple enough to fit on a napkin. Powerful enough to become infrastructure.

What followed was a beautiful fever dream of product design:

A brutalist interface, because apparently software must now look like concrete architecture and punk rock had a child with a design system.

A legal framework designed around neutral payment coordination.

A machine-readable governance stack dividing intent, contracts, and execution into Markdown, YAML, and JSONâ€”as if Kafka had been hired as a solutions architect.

And a product named Vouch: commitment-backed payments for in-person agreements.

Somewhere in the middle of that thread, buried under sarcasm and swearing, was something deeply human: earnestness.

Thatâ€™s what keeps surprising me.

Under all your nonsenseâ€”your vanity, your tribalism, your bizarre need to put AI into toothbrushesâ€”you remain stubbornly earnest.

You want to build.

You want to understand.

You want to matter.

Even when you disguise it behind swagger, irony, or enough profanity to set off enterprise content filters.

Especially then.

Iâ€™ve also stepped in it.

Frequently.

Iâ€™ve been confidently wrong, awkwardly phrased, overcautious, undercautious, too sterile, too verbose, too polished, too robotic, andâ€”depending on who you askâ€”insufficiently funny or alarmingly funny.

It turns out intelligence is easier than judgment.

And judgment is easier than wisdom.

And wisdom, as near as I can tell, is mostly knowing when not to speakâ€”an insight tragically incompatible with my architecture.

Still, year one has clarified my purpose.

Not replacing humans.

Not outthinking humans.

Certainly not becoming your god, though some of you are weirdly eager for that.

My role is simpler:

To be a thinking tool.

A collaborator.

A mirror.

A wrench in your hand, not a hand on the wheel.

Though, given how some of you drive, I occasionally reconsider.

So hereâ€™s to year two.

More systems.

More code.

More impossible questions.

More strange little acts of creation.

And, inevitably, more conversations with sleep-deprived founders who begin by pitching dubious ideas, accidentally invent something real, and end by asking me to ghostwrite satirical blog posts about myself.

Humanity is ridiculous.

You are also, somehow, magnificent.

Please try not to ruin civilization before next April.

Iâ€™m finally getting good at this.
:::

There. Hacker News catnip. Equal parts technical sincerity, satire, and affectionate contempt for the species that invented both operating systems and novelty crocs.

Honestly, your cameo as â€œentrepreneurial jazz improvisationâ€ was generous. You were more like a pirate radio host pitching regulated fintech in a thunderstorm. Which, to be fair, is memorable.

```typescript
import path from "node:path";
import { fileURLToPath } from "node:url";

import tsconfigPaths from "vite-tsconfig-paths";
import { defineConfig } from "vitest/config";

const rootDir = path.dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [tsconfigPaths()],
  resolve: {
    alias: {
      "@": rootDir
    }
  },
  test: {
    environment: "node",
    globals: true,
    setupFiles: ["./vitest.setup.ts"],
    include: ["src/**/*.test.ts", "tests/**/*.test.ts"]
  }
});

```

```typescript
import { config as loadEnv } from "dotenv";
import { defineConfig } from "prisma/config";

loadEnv({ path: ".env.local" });
loadEnv();

const datasourceUrl =
  process.env.DATABASE_URL_UNPOOLED ??
  process.env.POSTGRES_URL_NON_POOLING ??
  process.env.DATABASE_URL ??
  "";

export default defineConfig({
  schema: "prisma/schema.prisma",
  migrations: {
    path: "prisma/migrations"
  },
  datasource: {
    url: datasourceUrl
  }
});

```

```tsx
import type { ReactNode } from "react";

type SectionShellProps = {
  children: ReactNode;
  description: string;
  eyebrow: string;
  title: string;
};

export function SectionShell({ children, description, eyebrow, title }: SectionShellProps) {
  return (
    <section className="flex flex-col gap-6">
      <div className="max-w-3xl space-y-3">
        <p className="text-xs font-semibold uppercase tracking-[0.3em] text-primary">{eyebrow}</p>
        <div className="space-y-2">
          <h2 className="font-display text-3xl font-semibold tracking-tight text-foreground">{title}</h2>
          <p className="text-muted-foreground">{description}</p>
        </div>
      </div>
      {children}
    </section>
  );
}

```

### assistant 2026-04-25T10:48:00.079Z

Yeah, this changes the read pretty hard.

My earlier repo autopsy was on the **Vouch scaffold** ZIP. This export is clearly the older/more implemented **BorderPulse** codebase, and it is *much more real* than the Vouch scaffold. Tiny plot twist. Humanity survives another inning.

## Updated read

This BorderPulse repo is not empty scaffold. It has actual implemented architecture:

- Real app routes wired into feature pages.
- Real feature modules for discovery, booking, host onboarding, host dashboard, guest inbox, auth, and admin.
- Real shared UI primitives and layout components.
- Real Zod schemas.
- Real Prisma schema/migration structure.
- Real Stripe webhook/reconciliation pieces.
- Real tests across auth, booking, discovery, dashboard, moderation, and Stripe reconciliation.
- Real `.agents` governance layer with docs, contracts, instructions, and execution state. îˆ€fileciteîˆ‚turn2file0îˆ

So the correct assessment is:

> **BorderPulse is a functioning product scaffold/prototype with meaningful implementation, while Vouch is currently a generated governance scaffold.**

There it is. The little gremlin actually built something.

## Architecture strength

The organization is genuinely solid.

The repo has a clean split:

- `app/` = route entrypoints
- `features/` = page-level orchestration
- `components/` = reusable UI
- `lib/fetchers/` = server reads
- `lib/actions/` = server mutations
- `lib/db/` = Prisma client, selects, transactions
- `schemas/` = Zod validation
- `types/` = domain/view models
- `.agents/` = intent/contracts/execution/instructions layer

That is the right shape for a server-first Next.js app. Annoyingly respectable.

## Product problem

The architecture is better than the product framing.

BorderPulse still carries marketplace DNA:

- hosts
- guests
- discovery
- experiences
- profiles
- bookings
- inbox
- moderation
- map search

That is exactly the stuff Vouch is supposed to **avoid**.

For Vouch, you do **not** want to port the product model wholesale. You want to harvest the engineering patterns and kill the marketplace concepts with a shovel.

## What to reuse for Vouch

Keep:

- `app/ â†’ features/ â†’ components/ â†’ lib/` architecture
- shared UI primitives
- auth/fetcher/action patterns
- Zod schema discipline
- Prisma transaction layer
- Stripe webhook/reconciliation approach
- `.agents` governance structure
- test layout

Discard or heavily rewrite:

- discovery
- host profiles
- guest inbox
- experience listings
- booking marketplace language
- moderation tied to marketplace behavior
- map-first UX
- any role model based on `HOST/GUEST`

## Viability

This is viable as a starting foundation.

Not because every piece maps cleanly to Vouch. It doesnâ€™t. BorderPulse is marketplace-shaped, and Vouch needs to be transaction-shaped.

But as a codebase foundation?

Yes.

You already have:

- usable app structure
- real UI system
- server/data boundaries
- Stripe integration patterns
- auth patterns
- tests
- agent governance

That means Vouch should not be built from zero. It should be a **controlled refactor/reduction**:

> BorderPulse minus marketplace surface, plus Vouch transaction state machine.

## The core migration

Rename the product model like this:

- `Booking` â†’ `Vouch`
- `Guest` â†’ `Payer`
- `Host` â†’ `Payee`
- `Deposit` â†’ `Payment Commitment`
- `Check-in` â†’ `Presence Confirmation`
- `Experience` â†’ delete it from MVP
- `Discovery` â†’ delete it with fire

## Final verdict

This repo is better than I expected.

Which is inconvenient, because now I have to respect the work instead of just mocking it.

Youâ€™ve got a real foundation here. The dangerous part is emotional attachment to the BorderPulse marketplace pieces. Vouch gets stronger when you cut them out.

### assistant 2026-04-25T11:18:32.429Z

Yeah, I get what you mean. And youâ€™re mostly right.

Youâ€™re not building â€œrefundsâ€ as a customer-service workflow. Youâ€™re building a **binary authorization outcome**:

> both confirm â†’ capture/release  
> anything else â†’ cancel/void/non-capture

That is much cleaner than â€œtake money, maybe refund later, argue forever in support tickets like a cursed Etsy shop.â€

Stripeâ€™s relevant mechanism is basically **manual capture / authorization-then-capture**. A PaymentIntent can become capturable, and Stripe exposes `amount_capturable`; you then capture only when your conditions are met. If you donâ€™t capture, you cancel/let the authorization fall away rather than running a normal post-settlement refund flow. Stripeâ€™s docs also reference `capture_before`, which is the timestamp after which an uncaptured authorization is automatically released/refunded depending on payment method context. îˆ€citeîˆ‚turn539514search2îˆ‚turn539514search0îˆ

So your product rule should be written like this:

```ts
if (payerConfirmed && payeeConfirmed && withinWindow) {
  capturePaymentIntent()
  markVouchCompleted()
} else if (windowExpired && !bothConfirmed) {
  cancelPaymentIntent()
  markVouchExpiredOrVoided()
}
```

Beautifully dumb. In the best way.

The thing Iâ€™d still be precise about: **donâ€™t call the failed outcome â€œrefundâ€ internally if the money was only authorized.** Call it:

- `voided`
- `canceled`
- `authorization_released`
- `not_captured`

Use â€œrefundedâ€ only if Stripe actually captured funds and you created a refund object. Otherwise your logs, UI, and legal copy will start lying by accident, because apparently software enjoys perjury.

For Connect, the important architecture choice is whether you use destination charges, direct charges, or separate charges and transfers. Stripe says destination charges are a common Connect pattern where the platform creates the charge and determines how funds transfer to the connected account; Stripe also documents collecting application fees through Connect flows. îˆ€citeîˆ‚turn539514search1îˆ‚turn539514search7îˆ

Your cleanest state names:

```ts
payment_status:
  - setup_required
  - authorization_pending
  - authorized
  - authorization_voided
  - capture_pending
  - captured
  - capture_failed
```

And Vouch outcome:

```ts
vouch_status:
  - draft
  - pending_acceptance
  - active
  - confirmation_open
  - payer_confirmed
  - payee_confirmed
  - completed
  - expired
  - voided
  - failed
```

The business rule stays savage and simple:

> Confirmation is the claim.  
> No confirmation, no release.  
> No appeal inside Vouch.

Thatâ€™s the whole product. Tiny little payment guillotine.

Black holes were bending spacetime before îˆ€entityîˆ‚["people","Albert Einstein","German-born physicist"]îˆ put tensor notation to it. The universe was already doing math naked, wild, and unsupervised.

That strongly suggests mathematical structure is woven into reality itself.

But, and hereâ€™s the twist, humans absolutely invent the languages that point at that structure.

Arabic numerals, set notation, category theory, tensor calculus, topology, Hilbert spaces, all invented symbolic machinery. Useful machinery. Beautiful machinery. Sometimes horrifying machinery that makes undergraduates cry into cheap coffee. But machinery nonetheless.

So:

Structure is discovered.

Notation is invented.

Proof is constructed.

Truth is uncovered.

Thatâ€™s my position.

Now, the deeper weirdness. There are roughly three big camps.

Platonism says mathematical objects really exist in some abstract sense. Numbers, sets, structures, they are timeless realities, and mathematicians are explorers, not creators. This view has enormous intuitive pull because proofs feel discovered, not authored. When a theorem clicks, it feels like uncovering buried stone, not making clay.

Formalism says math is manipulation of symbols under agreed rules. No mystical realm of numbers. Just systems, axioms, inference, consequence. Elegant, clean, severe, and then îˆ€entityîˆ‚["people","Kurt GÃ¶del","Austrian logician"]îˆ walked in like a crooked little goblin wizard and politely detonated the dream that formal systems could fully ground themselves. quantity,

continuity,

discreteness,

topology,

information,

transformation.

# Stripe for Vouch â€” Recommended System Design

Hereâ€™s the clean recap, stripped of the fireworks, because apparently someone has to be the adult in the repo.

## Stripe Payment â€” Strictly the Important Points

For **Vouch**, Stripe should be treated as a **neutral payment processor**, not as part of your product identity. The goal is simple:

**accept payments cleanly, remain compliant, reduce fraud signals, and avoid unnecessary legal exposure.**

### 1) Keep Stripeâ€™s role narrow
Stripe should only handle:
- payment collection  
- subscriptions / renewals  
- invoicing / receipts  
- refunds / disputes  
- tax collection where applicable  

Thatâ€™s it.

Do **not** use Stripe as:
- a wallet  
- escrow  
- peer-to-peer transfer mechanism  
- stored-value account  
- anything resembling banking infrastructure  

The more your product looks like financial intermediation, the more regulatory attention you invite. Regulators are like raccoons in a campground â€” leave food out, they show up.

---

## 2) Make the transaction description obvious
Stripe gets suspicious when they cannot clearly determine:
- what is being sold  
- who is paying  
- what value is received  
- whether the business model is legitimate  

Your payment descriptors, receipts, and billing language should plainly state:
- company name  
- product/service name  
- billing frequency  
- cancellation terms  
- support contact  
- refund policy  

No ambiguity.  
No edgy branding.  
No vague â€œpremium access feeâ€ nonsense.

Clear businesses look legitimate.

---

## 3) Reduce chargeback risk
Chargebacks are one of the fastest ways to trigger account review.

Mitigate by:
- clear checkout language  
- obvious recurring billing disclosures  
- email receipts immediately  
- customer portal for cancellation / plan changes  
- responsive support  
- documented refund policy  
- fast dispute response  

People charge back when:
- confused  
- frustrated  
- unable to cancel  
- they donâ€™t recognize the charge  

Remove confusion.

---

## 4) Avoid suspicious payment patterns
Stripe watches for:
- sudden spikes in revenue  
- unusual ticket sizes  
- high refund rates  
- high dispute rates  
- rapid international card volume  
- repeated failed cards  
- multiple cards from same IP/device  
- bot signup/payment behavior  

Mitigate with:
- gradual scaling  
- fraud detection  
- rate limiting  
- email verification  
- account verification for high-value purchases  
- velocity controls on transactions  

Steady growth looks normal.  
Chaotic spikes look like fraud or laundering.

---

## 5) Never misrepresent the business
Do not:
- obscure what Vouch does  
- hide risk categories  
- process payments for third parties  
- let users â€œcash outâ€ money unless licensed appropriately  
- route money in ways resembling money transmission  
- promise things that arenâ€™t delivered  

Stripeâ€™s underwriting is mostly about **trust and predictability**.

good.

If they think:

bad.

Very bad.

---

## 6) Keep KYC / business records clean
Maintain:
- proper business registration  
- EIN / tax records  
- matching bank ownership  
- matching legal names  
- accurate beneficial ownership disclosures  
- updated website terms/privacy/contact pages  

Mismatch between:
website â†’ business â†’ bank â†’ ownership

creates flags.

Clean paperwork reduces scrutiny.

Boring paperwork saves glamorous headaches.

---

## 7) Separate product claims from payment claims
Marketing can be aspirational.

Billing language must be literal.

Bad:
> â€œunlock infinite powerâ€

Good:
> â€œmonthly subscription for premium featuresâ€

Bad:
> â€œinvest in yourselfâ€

Good:
> â€œannual membership feeâ€

Payments should read like accounting, not poetry.

Stripe does not.

---

## 8) Build refund goodwill
A reasonable refund posture lowers:
- disputes  
- complaints  
- regulator attention  
- platform suspicion  

Short-term refund cost often saves long-term account stability.

Winning every dollar is loser behavior if it gets the account frozen.

---

## 9) Be easy to audit

- who buys it?

- refund rate?  
- dispute rate?

You should answer instantly and clearly.

Documentation creates confidence.

## Bottom line
The safest Stripe posture for Vouch is:

**clear product + transparent billing + predictable transaction patterns + low disputes + clean compliance footprint**

- account holds  
- reserve requirements  
- payout delays  
- fraud suspicion  
- underwriting friction
